Sheet music enables musicians to learn, perform, and share music accurately across generations and cultures.\n\nHere are some key components of sheet music:\n\n1. **Staff**: A set of five horizontal lines and four spaces where notes and other musical symbols are placed.\n2. **Clef**: A symbol at the beginning of the staff indicating the pitch range, such as the treble clef (for higher pitches) or bass clef (for lower pitches).\n3. **Notes**: Symbols representing different pitches and durations.\n4. **Time Signature**: Two numbers at the beginning of a piece indicating the number of beats per measure and the type of note that receives one beat.\n5. **Key Signature**: Sharps or flats placed at the beginning of the staff to indicate the key of the piece.\n6. **Bar Lines**: Vertical lines dividing the staff into measures, each containing a specific number of beats.\n7. **Dynamics**: Symbols indicating the volume of the music, such as \"p\" for piano (soft) or \"f\" for forte (loud).\n8. **Tempo**: The speed of the music, often indicated with terms like \"Allegro\" (fast) or \"Largo\" (slow).\n9. **Articulation**: Symbols indicating how to play the notes, such as staccato (short and detached) or legato (smoothly connected).\n10. **Repeat Signs**: Symbols indicating repetition of a section of music.\n\nSheet music can vary depending on the instrument.
